Pages 1–10 Fact-Checked MCQs (394 Questions)

#7 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the current Governor of the Reserve Bank of India?

  • A. Sanjay Malhotra
  • B. Shaktikanta Das
  • C. Urjit Patel
  • D. Raghuram Rajan

Answer: Sanjay Malhotra

Sanjay Malhotra assumed charge as the 26th Governor of the Reserve Bank of India in December 2024, succeeding Shaktikanta Das. Prior to this role, he served as the Revenue Secretary in the Ministry of Finance.

#8 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Reserve Bank of India Act enacted?

  • A. 1934
  • B. 1935
  • C. 1949
  • D. 1955

Answer: 1934

The Reserve Bank of India Act was enacted in 1934 to provide the statutory framework for establishing India's central bank. Based on this legislation, the Reserve Bank of India commenced operations on 1 April 1935.

#9 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Banking Regulation Act enacted in India?

  • A. 1949
  • B. 1934
  • C. 1956
  • D. 1969

Answer: 1949

The Banking Regulation Act was passed in 1949 to regulate all banking firms in India and empower the Reserve Bank of India to license and supervise banks. It was initially enacted as the Banking Companies Act, 1949.

#10 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the State Bank of India founded after nationalizing the Imperial Bank of India?

  • A. 1 July 1955
  • B. 1 April 1935
  • C. 19 July 1969
  • D. 12 July 1982

Answer: 1 July 1955

The State Bank of India was established on 1 July 1955 through the State Bank of India Act, succeeding the Imperial Bank of India (founded in 1921). It is India's largest public sector commercial bank.

#11 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the State Bank of India located?

  • A. Mumbai
  • B. New Delhi
  • C. Kolkata
  • D. Chennai

Answer: Mumbai

The corporate headquarters of the State Bank of India is situated at Nariman Point in Mumbai, Maharashtra. It operates an extensive network of branches across India and overseas.

#12 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the Chairman of the State Bank of India?

  • A. Challa Sreenivasulu Setty
  • B. Dinesh Kumar Khara
  • C. Rajnish Kumar
  • D. Arundhati Bhattacharya

Answer: Challa Sreenivasulu Setty

Challa Sreenivasulu Setty took charge as the Chairman of the State Bank of India in August 2024, succeeding Dinesh Kumar Khara. He previously served as the senior-most Managing Director of the bank.

#13 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the official tagline of the State Bank of India?

  • A. Pure Banking Nothing Else
  • B. Together We Can
  • C. The Name You Can Bank Upon
  • D. Good People to Bank With

Answer: Pure Banking Nothing Else

The widely recognized corporate tagline of the State Bank of India is 'Pure Banking Nothing Else'. Other major taglines include 'With You All The Way' and 'The Banker to Every Indian'.

#14 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development founded?

  • A. 12 July 1982
  • B. 2 April 1990
  • C. 1 July 1955
  • D. 1 January 1982

Answer: 12 July 1982

The National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development was established on 12 July 1982 on the recommendations of the B. Sivaraman Committee. It acts as the apex development financial institution for agriculture and rural development in India.

#15 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development located?

  • A. Mumbai
  • B. Lucknow
  • C. New Delhi
  • D. Hyderabad

Answer: Mumbai

The headquarters of the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development is located at Bandra Kurla Complex in Mumbai, Maharashtra. It supervises Regional Rural Banks and Cooperative Banks across India.

#16 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the Chairman of the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development?

  • A. Shaji K. V.
  • B. Govinda Rajulu Chintala
  • C. Harsh Kumar Bhanwala
  • D. Manoj Mittal

Answer: Shaji K. V.

Shaji K. V. serves as the Chairman of the National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development, having assumed office in December 2022. He previously worked as Deputy Managing Director of the institution.

#17 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the Small Industries Development Bank of India founded?

  • A. 2 April 1990
  • B. 12 July 1982
  • C. 9 July 1988
  • D. 12 April 1988

Answer: 2 April 1990

The Small Industries Development Bank of India was established on 2 April 1990 under an Act of the Indian Parliament. It serves as the principal financial institution for the promotion, financing, and development of Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises.

#18 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the Small Industries Development Bank of India located?

  • A. Lucknow
  • B. Mumbai
  • C. New Delhi
  • D. Bengaluru

Answer: Lucknow

The head office of the Small Industries Development Bank of India is located in Lucknow, Uttar Pradesh. It provides refinance facilities and direct credit support to small-scale industries nationwide.

#19 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the Chairman and Managing Director of the Small Industries Development Bank of India?

  • A. Manoj Mittal
  • B. Sivasubramanian Ramann
  • C. Mohammad Mustafa
  • D. Shaji K. V.

Answer: Manoj Mittal

Manoj Mittal serves as the Chairman and Managing Director of the Small Industries Development Bank of India. Before this appointment, he served as Managing Director of the Industrial Finance Corporation of India.

#20 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the National Housing Bank founded?

  • A. 1988
  • B. 1982
  • C. 1990
  • D. 1999

Answer: 1988

The National Housing Bank was set up on 9 July 1988 under the National Housing Bank Act, 1987. It is wholly owned by the Government of India and acts as the apex institution for housing finance.

#21 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the National Housing Bank located?

  • A. New Delhi
  • B. Mumbai
  • C. Lucknow
  • D. Hyderabad

Answer: New Delhi

The headquarters of the National Housing Bank is located in New Delhi. It registers and supervises Housing Finance Companies and promotes inclusive housing credit.

#22 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Export-Import Bank of India founded?

  • A. 1982
  • B. 1988
  • C. 1990
  • D. 1976

Answer: 1982

The Export-Import Bank of India was established in 1982 under the Export-Import Bank of India Act, 1981. It finances, facilitates, and promotes India's international trade and cross-border investments.

#23 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the Export-Import Bank of India located?

  • A. Mumbai
  • B. New Delhi
  • C. Kolkata
  • D. Chennai

Answer: Mumbai

The headquarters of the Export-Import Bank of India is located at Maker Chambers in Mumbai, Maharashtra. It extends lines of credit to overseas financial institutions and sovereign governments.

#24 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the Securities and Exchange Board of India founded, and when did it receive statutory status?

  • A. Founded 12 April 1988; Statutory status in 1992
  • B. Founded 1992; Statutory status in 1995
  • C. Founded 1982; Statutory status in 1988
  • D. Founded 1999; Statutory status in 2000

Answer: Founded 12 April 1988; Statutory status in 1992

The Securities and Exchange Board of India was constituted as a non-statutory body on 12 April 1988 and received statutory powers on 30 January 1992 through the Securities and Exchange Board of India Act, 1992. It regulates securities and commodity markets in India.

#25 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the Securities and Exchange Board of India located?

  • A. Mumbai
  • B. New Delhi
  • C. Ahmedabad
  • D. Kolkata

Answer: Mumbai

The headquarters of the Securities and Exchange Board of India is located at Bandra Kurla Complex in Mumbai, Maharashtra. It also maintains regional offices in New Delhi, Kolkata, Chennai, and Ahmedabad.

#26 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the Chairman of the Securities and Exchange Board of India?

  • A. Tuhin Kanta Pandey
  • B. Madhabi Puri Buch
  • C. Ajay Tyagi
  • D. Upendra Kumar Sinha

Answer: Tuhin Kanta Pandey

Tuhin Kanta Pandey assumed charge as Chairman of the Securities and Exchange Board of India on 1 March 2025 for a three-year term, succeeding Madhabi Puri Buch. He previously served as Union Finance Secretary.

#27 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India founded?

  • A. 1999
  • B. 2000
  • C. 1992
  • D. 2003

Answer: 1999

The Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India was constituted in 1999 following the recommendations of the Malhotra Committee report and incorporated as a statutory body in April 2000. It regulates and promotes the insurance industry in India.

#28 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India located?

  • A. Hyderabad
  • B. Mumbai
  • C. New Delhi
  • D. Bengaluru

Answer: Hyderabad

The headquarters of the Insurance Regulatory and Development Authority of India is located in Hyderabad, Telangana, having shifted there from New Delhi in 2001. It oversees life, general, and health insurers.

#29 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority founded?

  • A. 2003
  • B. 1999
  • C. 2008
  • D. 2014

Answer: 2003

The Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority was established by the Government of India in 2003 to promote old-age income security and later gained statutory status under the 2013 Act. It administers the National Pension System and Atal Pension Yojana.

#30 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Where is the headquarters of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority located?

  • A. New Delhi
  • B. Mumbai
  • C. Hyderabad
  • D. Pune

Answer: New Delhi

The headquarters of the Pension Fund Regulatory and Development Authority is located in New Delhi. It regulates pension funds and protects the interests of subscribers across India.

#31 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the maximum deposit insurance coverage provided by the Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation per depositor per bank?

  • A. Rupees 5 Lakh
  • B. Rupees 1 Lakh
  • C. Rupees 2 Lakh
  • D. Rupees 10 Lakh

Answer: Rupees 5 Lakh

The Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation insures principal and interest up to a maximum limit of Rupees 5 Lakh per depositor per bank in the same right and capacity. This limit was raised from Rupees 1 Lakh in February 2020.

#32 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

The Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation is a wholly-owned subsidiary of which institution?

  • A. Reserve Bank of India
  • B. State Bank of India
  • C. Ministry of Finance
  • D. National Bank for Agriculture and Rural Development

Answer: Reserve Bank of India

The Deposit Insurance and Credit Guarantee Corporation is a wholly-owned subsidiary of the Reserve Bank of India established in 1978. It covers commercial banks, including Local Area Banks, Payments Banks, Small Finance Banks, and Regional Rural Banks.

#33 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)Fact-Check Updated

What is the Cash Reserve Ratio cited in the study material versus the current rate as of November 2026?

  • A. PDF states 4%; Current rate as of November 2026 is 3.00%
  • B. PDF states 4%; Current rate is 4.50%
  • C. PDF states 3%; Current rate is 4.00%
  • D. PDF states 5%; Current rate is 3.50%

Answer: PDF states 4%; Current rate as of November 2026 is 3.00%

UPDATED FACT-CHECK: The PDF lists Cash Reserve Ratio as 4%, whereas as per the Reserve Bank of India Monetary Policy in October–November 2026, the Cash Reserve Ratio is 3.00% of Net Demand and Time Liabilities. Banks must maintain this cash balance directly with the Reserve Bank of India.

#34 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the current Statutory Liquidity Ratio required to be maintained by commercial banks?

  • A. 18%
  • B. 15%
  • C. 20%
  • D. 4%

Answer: 18%

The Statutory Liquidity Ratio is currently 18.00% of a bank's Net Demand and Time Liabilities. Banks maintain this reserve themselves in the form of liquid cash, gold, or approved government securities.

#35 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)Fact-Check Updated

What is the Policy Repo Rate given in the PDF versus the current rate as of November 2026?

  • A. PDF states 5.25%; Current rate as of November 2026 is 5.50%
  • B. PDF states 5.25%; Current rate is 6.50%
  • C. PDF states 6.00%; Current rate is 5.25%
  • D. PDF states 5.50%; Current rate is 5.00%

Answer: PDF states 5.25%; Current rate as of November 2026 is 5.50%

UPDATED FACT-CHECK: The PDF lists the Policy Repo Rate as 5.25%, whereas the Reserve Bank of India Monetary Policy Committee raised it by 25 basis points to 5.50% in October 2026 (with Standing Deposit Facility at 5.25%). Repo Rate is the rate at which the central bank lends short-term funds to commercial banks against government securities.

#36 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the fixed Reverse Repo Rate of the Reserve Bank of India?

  • A. 3.35%
  • B. 3.75%
  • C. 4.00%
  • D. 5.25%

Answer: 3.35%

The fixed Reverse Repo Rate stands at 3.35%, while the Standing Deposit Facility rate (5.25%) serves as the operative floor of the Liquidity Adjustment Facility corridor. Reverse Repo is the rate at which the central bank absorbs liquidity from commercial banks.

#37 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

How is the Marginal Standing Facility rate calculated in relation to the Policy Repo Rate?

  • A. Policy Repo Rate plus 0.25%
  • B. Policy Repo Rate minus 0.25%
  • C. Policy Repo Rate plus 0.50%
  • D. Equal to Policy Repo Rate

Answer: Policy Repo Rate plus 0.25%

The Marginal Standing Facility rate is pegged at 25 basis points (0.25%) above the Policy Repo Rate, standing at 5.75% when the Repo Rate is 5.50%. It allows scheduled commercial banks to borrow overnight funds in emergencies by dipping into their Statutory Liquidity Ratio portfolio.

#38 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

To which policy rate is the Bank Rate of the Reserve Bank of India aligned?

  • A. Same as Marginal Standing Facility Rate
  • B. Same as Reverse Repo Rate
  • C. Same as Cash Reserve Ratio
  • D. Same as Standing Deposit Facility Rate

Answer: Same as Marginal Standing Facility Rate

The Bank Rate is aligned with the Marginal Standing Facility rate (currently 5.75%) and acts as the penal rate charged on banks for shortfalls in reserve requirements. It is the standard rate at which the Reserve Bank of India buys or rediscounts bills of exchange.

#39 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Which lending benchmark system replaced the Base Rate system in India in April 2016?

  • A. Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate
  • B. Benchmark Prime Lending Rate
  • C. External Benchmark Lending Rate
  • D. Statutory Liquidity Ratio

Answer: Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate

The Reserve Bank of India introduced the Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate effective 1 April 2016 to replace the Base Rate system. It was designed to improve monetary policy transmission and bring transparency to bank lending rates.

#40 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the full form of the banking acronym MCLR?

  • A. Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate
  • B. Minimum Cost of Funds based Lending Rate
  • C. Marginal Credit of Funds based Loan Rate
  • D. Monetary Cost of Funds based Lending Rate

Answer: Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate

MCLR stands for Marginal Cost of Funds based Lending Rate, which is the minimum internal benchmark interest rate below which a bank cannot lend. It is calculated based on marginal cost of funds, operating costs, negative carry on Cash Reserve Ratio, and tenure premium.

#41 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending target for Domestic Scheduled Commercial Banks?

  • A. 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 32% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 50% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Domestic Scheduled Commercial Banks are mandated to lend 40% of their Adjusted Net Bank Credit or Credit Equivalent Amount of Off-Balance Sheet Exposure, whichever is higher, to priority sectors. Priority sectors include agriculture, micro-enterprises, education, housing, and weaker sections.

#42 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending target for Foreign Banks with 20 branches and above in India?

  • A. 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 32% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 25% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Foreign banks operating 20 or more branches in India must achieve a Priority Sector Lending target of 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit, on par with domestic banks. Foreign banks with fewer than 20 branches also have a 40% target with flexible sub-targets.

#43 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the overall Priority Sector Lending target for Small Finance Banks?

  • A. 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 50% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 60% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Small Finance Banks are required to extend 75% of their Adjusted Net Bank Credit to sectors eligible for classification as Priority Sector Lending. This high mandate ensures credit penetration among unserved and underserved segments.

#44 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the overall Priority Sector Lending target for Regional Rural Banks?

  • A. 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 40% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 60% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 50% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 75% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Regional Rural Banks have an overall Priority Sector Lending target of 75% of their total outstanding advances. This requirement reinforces their core mandate of supporting rural artisans, small farmers, and village industries.

#45 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending sub-target allocated to Agriculture for domestic banks?

  • A. 18% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 7.5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 18% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Within the overall 40% Priority Sector Lending mandate, domestic commercial banks must allocate at least 18% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit to the Agriculture sector. This covers farm credit, agriculture infrastructure, and ancillary activities.

#46 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending sub-target for Small and Marginal Farmers within the agriculture quota?

  • A. 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 8% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 7.5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Within the 18% agriculture lending target, a dedicated sub-target of 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit is prescribed for Small and Marginal Farmers. Marginal farmers hold up to 1 hectare of land, while small farmers hold between 1 and 2 hectares.

#47 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending sub-target prescribed for Micro Enterprises?

  • A. 7.5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 7.5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Domestic commercial banks and Small Finance Banks must allocate at least 7.5% of their Adjusted Net Bank Credit to Micro Enterprises. This ensures steady working capital and term loan access for grassroots businesses.

#48 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the Priority Sector Lending sub-target allocated to Weaker Sections for domestic banks?

  • A. 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • B. 10% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • C. 15% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit
  • D. 7.5% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

Answer: 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit

The Priority Sector Lending sub-target for Weaker Sections is 12% of Adjusted Net Bank Credit for domestic commercial banks (and 15% for Regional Rural Banks). Beneficiaries include Scheduled Castes, Scheduled Tribes, self-help groups, and distressed farmers.

#49 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

After how many days of overdue interest or principal installment is a loan classified as a Non-Performing Asset?

  • A. 90 days
  • B. 30 days
  • C. 60 days
  • D. 180 days

Answer: 90 days

A loan or advance becomes a Non-Performing Asset when interest or principal installment remains overdue for a period of more than 90 days. Once classified as non-performing, banks stop accruing interest income on that account.

#50 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

For what duration does a Non-Performing Asset remain classified as a Sub-standard Asset?

  • A. Up to 12 months
  • B. More than 12 months
  • C. Up to 90 days
  • D. Up to 24 months

Answer: Up to 12 months

A Sub-standard Asset is one which has remained a Non-Performing Asset for a period less than or equal to 12 months. Such assets carry well-defined credit weaknesses that jeopardize the liquidation of the debt.

#51 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When is a Non-Performing Asset classified into the Doubtful Asset category?

  • A. When it remains in the sub-standard category for more than 12 months
  • B. When it is overdue for 90 days
  • C. When it is declared uncollectible by auditors
  • D. When it is overdue for 6 months

Answer: When it remains in the sub-standard category for more than 12 months

An asset is classified as a Doubtful Asset if it has remained in the sub-standard category for a period exceeding 12 months. Full collection or liquidation in such accounts is highly questionable and improbable.

#52 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

How is a Loss Asset defined under Reserve Bank of India asset classification norms?

  • A. An uncollectible asset identified by the bank, auditors, or Reserve Bank of India inspection
  • B. A loan overdue for 90 days
  • C. A Non-Performing Asset up to 12 months
  • D. A restructured standard loan

Answer: An uncollectible asset identified by the bank, auditors, or Reserve Bank of India inspection

A Loss Asset is one where loss has been identified by the bank, internal or external auditors, or the Reserve Bank of India inspection, but the amount has not been written off wholly. Such an asset is considered uncollectible and of negligible salvage value.

#53 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act enacted?

  • A. 2002
  • B. 2016
  • C. 1993
  • D. 2005

Answer: 2002

The Securitisation and Reconstruction of Financial Assets and Enforcement of Security Interest Act was enacted in 2002. It empowers banks and financial institutions to auction residential or commercial collateral properties to recover loans without court intervention.

#54 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code enacted in India?

  • A. 2016
  • B. 2002
  • C. 2014
  • D. 2019

Answer: 2016

The Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code was enacted in 2016 to consolidate and amend laws relating to reorganization and insolvency resolution of corporate persons, partnership firms, and individuals in a time-bound manner.

#55 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the maximum overall timeline permitted for completing the Corporate Insolvency Resolution Process under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code?

  • A. 330 days
  • B. 180 days
  • C. 270 days
  • D. 90 days

Answer: 330 days

Under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code (Amendment) Act, 2019, the Corporate Insolvency Resolution Process must be mandatorily completed within 330 days, including any extension granted and time taken in legal proceedings.

#56 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Which quasi-judicial body deals with corporate insolvency resolution and company law disputes in India?

  • A. National Company Law Tribunal
  • B. Debt Recovery Tribunal
  • C. Securities Appellate Tribunal
  • D. National Consumer Disputes Redressal Commission

Answer: National Company Law Tribunal

The National Company Law Tribunal acts as the Adjudicating Authority for insolvency resolution and liquidation of corporate persons under the Insolvency and Bankruptcy Code, 2016. Appeals against its orders lie before the National Company Law Appellate Tribunal.

#57 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the numerical range of a Credit Information Bureau (India) Limited credit score?

  • A. 300 to 900
  • B. 100 to 1000
  • C. 0 to 500
  • D. 200 to 800

Answer: 300 to 900

Credit Information Bureau (India) Limited calculates credit scores on a three-digit scale ranging from 300 to 900. A higher score reflects stronger creditworthiness and responsible repayment behavior.

#58 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What score threshold is generally considered a good Credit Information Bureau (India) Limited credit score by lenders?

  • A. 750 and above
  • B. 500 and above
  • C. 600 and above
  • D. 450 and above

Answer: 750 and above

A credit score of 750 or above is widely regarded by banks and non-banking financial companies as an ideal credit score. Applicants with scores above 750 enjoy faster loan approvals and competitive interest rates.

#59 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the National Electronic Funds Transfer system started in India?

  • A. 2005
  • B. 2010
  • C. 2016
  • D. 2008

Answer: 2005

The National Electronic Funds Transfer system was introduced by the Reserve Bank of India in November 2005. Since December 2019, it operates 24 hours a day, 7 days a week across 48 half-hourly settlement batches.

#60 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the minimum transaction limit for Real Time Gross Settlement in India?

  • A. Rupees 2 Lakh
  • B. Rupees 1 Lakh
  • C. Rupees 5 Lakh
  • D. Rupees 50,000

Answer: Rupees 2 Lakh

The minimum amount to be remitted through Real Time Gross Settlement is Rupees 2 Lakh, with no upper or maximum ceiling. It settles high-value interbank and customer transactions individually on a real-time basis.

#61 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the maximum per-transaction limit for Immediate Payment Service?

  • A. Rupees 5 Lakh
  • B. Rupees 2 Lakh
  • C. Rupees 1 Lakh
  • D. Rupees 10 Lakh

Answer: Rupees 5 Lakh

The Reserve Bank of India increased the per-transaction limit for Immediate Payment Service from Rupees 2 Lakh to Rupees 5 Lakh. Operated by the National Payments Corporation of India, it enables instant 24×7 electronic fund transfers.

#62 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the Unified Payments Interface launched by the National Payments Corporation of India?

  • A. 2016
  • B. 2014
  • C. 2012
  • D. 2018

Answer: 2016

The Unified Payments Interface was launched in April 2016 by the National Payments Corporation of India. It powers multiple bank accounts into a single mobile application, merging several banking features and seamless merchant payments.

#63 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the National Payments Corporation of India founded and where is its headquarters?

  • A. Founded in 2008; Headquarters in Mumbai
  • B. Founded in 2016; Headquarters in New Delhi
  • C. Founded in 2005; Headquarters in Bengaluru
  • D. Founded in 2010; Headquarters in Hyderabad

Answer: Founded in 2008; Headquarters in Mumbai

The National Payments Corporation of India was incorporated in December 2008 as a not-for-profit organization under Section 8 of the Companies Act, with its headquarters in Mumbai. It serves as the umbrella organization for operating retail payments and settlement systems in India.

#64 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

Who is the Managing Director and Chief Executive Officer of the National Payments Corporation of India?

  • A. Dilip Asbe
  • B. A. P. Hota
  • C. Biswamohan Mahapatra
  • D. Ajay Kumar Choudhary

Answer: Dilip Asbe

Dilip Asbe serves as the Managing Director and Chief Executive Officer of the National Payments Corporation of India. He played a pivotal role in architecting and scaling the Unified Payments Interface platform.

#65 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

When was the Bharat Interface for Money mobile application launched?

  • A. December 2016
  • B. April 2016
  • C. August 2014
  • D. November 2017

Answer: December 2016

The Bharat Interface for Money mobile application was launched by Prime Minister Narendra Modi on 30 December 2016. Developed by the National Payments Corporation of India, it facilitates direct bank-to-bank payments using the Unified Payments Interface.

#66 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

In which year was the domestic RuPay card payment network launched in India?

  • A. 2012
  • B. 2016
  • C. 2008
  • D. 2014

Answer: 2012

RuPay, India's indigenous card payment network, was conceived and launched by the National Payments Corporation of India in March 2012. It fulfills the Reserve Bank of India's vision of a domestic, open-loop multilateral payment system.

#67 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the full form of the payment system NACH?

  • A. National Automated Clearing House
  • B. National Automatic Credit Hub
  • C. National Account Clearing House
  • D. National Automated Cash Handling

Answer: National Automated Clearing House

NACH stands for National Automated Clearing House, a centralized web-based payment solution operated by the National Payments Corporation of India. It consolidated multiple regional Electronic Clearing Service systems across the country.

#68 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

For what primary purpose is the National Automated Clearing House used?

  • A. Bulk recurring payments such as salaries, Equated Monthly Installments, pensions, and subsidies
  • B. High-value real-time gross interbank settlements
  • C. International cross-border wire transfers
  • D. Issuing physical demand drafts

Answer: Bulk recurring payments such as salaries, Equated Monthly Installments, pensions, and subsidies

The National Automated Clearing House is designed for high-volume, repetitive electronic transactions like salary disbursements, dividend payouts, pensions, utility bill collections, and loan Equated Monthly Installments.

#69 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the full form of the cheque clearing mechanism CTS?

  • A. Cheque Truncation System
  • B. Central Transaction System
  • C. Cheque Transfer Service
  • D. Clearing Truncation Standard

Answer: Cheque Truncation System

CTS stands for Cheque Truncation System, an online image-based cheque clearing process implemented by the Reserve Bank of India. It eliminates the physical movement of paper cheques from the presenting bank to the drawee bank branch.

#70 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

What is the full form of MICR printed at the bottom of bank cheques?

  • A. Magnetic Ink Character Recognition
  • B. Magnetic Internal Code Reader
  • C. Machine Ink Character Recognition
  • D. Magnetic Identification Code Registry

Answer: Magnetic Ink Character Recognition

MICR stands for Magnetic Ink Character Recognition, a 9-digit numeric code printed using special magnetic ink at the bottom of cheques. The first three digits represent the city, the next three represent the bank, and the last three identify the branch.

#71 • Page 1 (Questions 7–71)

How many alphanumeric characters does an Indian Financial System Code contain, and what is its structure?

  • A. 11 characters (4 Bank code + 0 + 6 Branch code)
  • B. 9 digits (3 City + 3 Bank + 3 Branch)
  • C. 8 or 11 characters
  • D. 15 alphanumeric characters

Answer: 11 characters (4 Bank code + 0 + 6 Branch code)

The Indian Financial System Code is an 11-character alphanumeric code assigned by the Reserve Bank of India to identify bank branches participating in electronic fund transfers. Its first four characters denote the bank, the fifth character is always zero, and the last six characters specify the branch.

#72 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

How many characters does a Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication code contain?

  • A. 8 or 11 characters
  • B. 11 characters only
  • C. 9 digits
  • D. 12 characters

Answer: 8 or 11 characters

A Society for Worldwide Interbank Financial Telecommunication code (also known as a Bank Identifier Code) consists of either 8 or 11 alphanumeric characters. An 8-character code identifies the primary head office, while an 11-character code specifies a particular branch.

#73 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What do the first two digits of a 15-digit Goods and Services Tax Identification Number denote?

  • A. State Code
  • B. Permanent Account Number
  • C. Entity Number
  • D. Check Digit

Answer: State Code

The Goods and Services Tax Identification Number is a 15-character alphanumeric identifier in which the first two digits represent the State Code as per the Indian Census 2011. Characters 3 to 12 contain the taxpayer's Permanent Account Number.

#74 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

In a 10-character Permanent Account Number, what does the fourth character denote?

  • A. Status of the holder (for example, P stands for Individual)
  • B. First letter of the holder's surname
  • C. State of registration
  • D. Year of issuance

Answer: Status of the holder (for example, P stands for Individual)

The fourth character of a 10-character Permanent Account Number indicates the category or status of the taxpayer, such as 'P' for Individual, 'C' for Company, 'F' for Firm, and 'T' for Trust. The fifth character represents the first letter of the holder's surname or entity name.

#75 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Which statutory authority issues the 12-digit Aadhaar unique identification number, and in which year was it established?

  • A. Unique Identification Authority of India (2009)
  • B. National Informatics Centre (2005)
  • C. Registrar General of India (2011)
  • D. Reserve Bank of India (2008)

Answer: Unique Identification Authority of India (2009)

Aadhaar is issued by the Unique Identification Authority of India, which was established by the Government of India in January 2009 and later given statutory backing under the Aadhaar Act, 2016. Each Aadhaar number is a random 12-digit biometric and demographic identifier.

#76 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Where is the headquarters of the Unique Identification Authority of India located?

  • A. New Delhi
  • B. Mumbai
  • C. Bengaluru
  • D. Hyderabad

Answer: New Delhi

The headquarters of the Unique Identification Authority of India is located in New Delhi. It also operates eight regional offices across major Indian cities to manage enrollment and authentication services.

#77 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the full form of KYC in banking compliance?

  • A. Know Your Customer
  • B. Keep Your Credit
  • C. Know Your Capital
  • D. Key Yield Calculation

Answer: Know Your Customer

KYC stands for Know Your Customer, a mandatory regulatory due diligence process used by banks and financial institutions to verify the identity and address of clients. It helps prevent identity theft, financial fraud, and illicit fund flows.

#78 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the full form of AML in financial regulation?

  • A. Anti-Money Laundering
  • B. Asset Management Liability
  • C. Automated Monetary Ledger
  • D. Anti-Market Liquidation

Answer: Anti-Money Laundering

AML stands for Anti-Money Laundering, referring to the laws, regulations, and procedures designed to prevent criminals from disguising illegally obtained funds as legitimate income. Banks must report suspicious transactions under these frameworks.

#79 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

In which year was the Prevention of Money Laundering Act enacted, and when did it come into force?

  • A. Enacted in 2002; Effective from 2005
  • B. Enacted in 2005; Effective from 2008
  • C. Enacted in 2000; Effective from 2002
  • D. Enacted in 2016; Effective from 2017

Answer: Enacted in 2002; Effective from 2005

The Prevention of Money Laundering Act was enacted in 2002 to combat money laundering and provide for confiscation of property derived from criminal activities. Its provisions came into force on 1 July 2005.

#80 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Under which Union Ministry does the Financial Intelligence Unit – India operate?

  • A. Ministry of Finance
  • B. Ministry of Home Affairs
  • C. Ministry of Corporate Affairs
  • D. Ministry of External Affairs

Answer: Ministry of Finance

The Financial Intelligence Unit – India operates under the Department of Revenue, Ministry of Finance, Government of India. Set up in November 2004, it is the central national agency responsible for receiving, analyzing, and disseminating suspicious financial transaction reports.

#81 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Who was appointed as the Director of the Financial Intelligence Unit – India in July 2026?

  • A. Satya Pal Kumar
  • B. Pankaj Kumar Mishra
  • C. Vivek Aggarwal
  • D. Sanjay Kumar Mishra

Answer: Satya Pal Kumar

Indian Revenue Service officer Satya Pal Kumar was appointed as Director of the Financial Intelligence Unit – India in July 2026, succeeding Pankaj Kumar Mishra. He leads national financial intelligence efforts against money laundering and terror financing.

#82 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What are the three sequential stages of Money Laundering?

  • A. Placement, Layering, and Integration
  • B. Collection, Transfer, and Withdrawal
  • C. Deposit, Lending, and Recovery
  • D. Origination, Securitization, and Settlement

Answer: Placement, Layering, and Integration

Money laundering typically occurs in three stages: Placement (introducing illicit cash into the financial system), Layering (concealing the source through complex transactions), and Integration (reintroducing laundered funds into the legitimate economy).

#83 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the minimum paid-up voting equity capital required to set up a Small Finance Bank in India?

  • A. Rupees 200 Crore
  • B. Rupees 100 Crore
  • C. Rupees 500 Crore
  • D. Rupees 300 Crore

Answer: Rupees 200 Crore

Under Reserve Bank of India guidelines for on-tap licensing, the minimum paid-up voting equity capital requirement for a Small Finance Bank is Rupees 200 Crore. For Urban Co-operative Banks transitioning voluntarily into a Small Finance Bank, the initial requirement is Rupees 100 Crore, rising to Rupees 200 Crore within five years.

#84 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the maximum end-of-day deposit balance allowed per individual customer in a Payments Bank?

  • A. Rupees 2 Lakh
  • B. Rupees 1 Lakh
  • C. Rupees 5 Lakh
  • D. Rupees 50,000

Answer: Rupees 2 Lakh

The Reserve Bank of India enhanced the maximum end-of-day deposit limit per individual customer for Payments Banks from Rupees 1 Lakh to Rupees 2 Lakh. Payments Banks accept demand deposits and provide remittance services.

#85 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Are Payments Banks permitted to undertake lending activities or issue credit cards in India?

  • A. No, they cannot lend or issue credit cards
  • B. Yes, up to Rupees 50,000
  • C. Yes, only to Priority Sector borrowers
  • D. Yes, without any restriction

Answer: No, they cannot lend or issue credit cards

Payments Banks are strictly prohibited from undertaking lending activities or issuing credit cards under Reserve Bank of India licensing norms. They can issue Automated Teller Machine or debit cards, accept deposits up to Rupees 2 Lakh, and distribute third-party financial products.

#86 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Which entity became the first Payments Bank to commence live operations in India?

  • A. Airtel Payments Bank
  • B. India Post Payments Bank
  • C. Paytm Payments Bank
  • D. Fino Payments Bank

Answer: Airtel Payments Bank

Airtel Payments Bank became the first Payments Bank in India to roll out live banking services, launching pilot operations in November 2016 and nationwide services in January 2017.

#87 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Which bank became the first Small Finance Bank to start operations in India in 2016?

  • A. Capital Small Finance Bank
  • B. AU Small Finance Bank
  • C. Equitas Small Finance Bank
  • D. Ujjivan Small Finance Bank

Answer: Capital Small Finance Bank

Capital Small Finance Bank (formerly Capital Local Area Bank) became India's first Small Finance Bank when it commenced operations on 24 April 2016 in Jalandhar, Punjab.

#88 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

In which year was the Regional Rural Banks Act enacted in India?

  • A. 1976
  • B. 1975
  • C. 1982
  • D. 1969

Answer: 1976

The Regional Rural Banks Act was passed in 1976 to replace an ordinance promulgated on 26 September 1975, following the recommendations of the Narasimham Working Group. Prathama Bank was the first Regional Rural Bank established in India.

#89 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the statutory shareholding pattern of a Regional Rural Bank among the Central Government, State Government, and Sponsor Bank?

  • A. Central Government 50%, State Government 15%, Sponsor Bank 35%
  • B. Central Government 35%, State Government 15%, Sponsor Bank 50%
  • C. Central Government 50%, State Government 25%, Sponsor Bank 25%
  • D. Central Government 60%, State Government 20%, Sponsor Bank 20%

Answer: Central Government 50%, State Government 15%, Sponsor Bank 35%

The issued capital of a Regional Rural Bank is subscribed in the fixed statutory ratio of 50% by the Central Government, 15% by the concerned State Government, and 35% by the Sponsor Commercial Bank.

#90 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

In which year was the Lead Bank Scheme introduced by the Reserve Bank of India?

  • A. 1969
  • B. 1975
  • C. 1982
  • D. 1991

Answer: 1969

The Lead Bank Scheme was introduced by the Reserve Bank of India in December 1969 based on the recommendations of the Gadgil Study Group and Nariman Committee. Under this scheme, a designated bank coordinates credit and banking development in each district.

#91 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

Who chairs the State Level Bankers' Committee meetings in an Indian state?

  • A. Chairman or Managing Director of the Convenor Bank
  • B. Governor of the State
  • C. Regional Director of Reserve Bank of India
  • D. State Finance Minister

Answer: Chairman or Managing Director of the Convenor Bank

The State Level Bankers' Committee is an apex inter-institutional forum at the state level chaired by the Chairman and Managing Director or Executive Director of the designated Convenor Bank. It coordinates the implementation of government development programs and credit plans.

#92 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

When was the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana launched?

  • A. 28 August 2014
  • B. 2 October 2014
  • C. 9 May 2015
  • D. 8 April 2015

Answer: 28 August 2014

The Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana, India's flagship National Mission for Financial Inclusion, was launched by Prime Minister Narendra Modi on 28 August 2014. It provides universal access to basic savings bank accounts, RuPay debit cards, and micro-insurance.

#93 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the maximum overdraft facility limit available to eligible account holders under the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana?

  • A. Rupees 10,000
  • B. Rupees 5,000
  • C. Rupees 20,000
  • D. Rupees 2,000

Answer: Rupees 10,000

Under the Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana, the overdraft facility limit was doubled from Rupees 5,000 to Rupees 10,000 for eligible account holders after six months of satisfactory account operation. An overdraft of up to Rupees 2,000 is available without strict conditions.

For Pradhan Mantri Jan Dhan Yojana accounts opened after 28 August 2018, the accidental death insurance cover on RuPay debit cards was enhanced from Rupees 1 Lakh to Rupees 2 Lakh.

The 'Tarun' category covers loans above Rupees 5 Lakh and up to Rupees 10 Lakh for established micro and small enterprises looking for significant business expansion.

#99 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the loan range introduced in 2024 under the 'Tarun Plus' category of the Pradhan Mantri Micro Units Development and Refinance Agency Yojana?

Answer: Above Rupees 10 Lakh and up to Rupees 20 Lakh

Announced in the Union Budget 2024–25 and notified in October 2024, the 'Tarun Plus' category offers loans above Rupees 10 Lakh and up to Rupees 20 Lakh for entrepreneurs who have previously availed and successfully repaid loans under the Tarun category.

Answer: 23 September 2018 in Ranchi, Jharkhand

The Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana was launched by Prime Minister Narendra Modi on 23 September 2018 in Ranchi, Jharkhand. It is the world's largest publicly funded health assurance scheme.

#102 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the annual health insurance coverage provided per eligible family under the Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana?

  • B. Rupees 2 Lakh per family per year
  • C. Rupees 10 Lakh per family per year
  • D. Rupees 3 Lakh per family per year

Answer: Rupees 5 Lakh per family per year

The Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana provides cashless health coverage of up to Rupees 5 Lakh per family per year for secondary and tertiary care hospitalization across empaneled public and private hospitals.

#103 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

How many days of pre-hospitalization and post-hospitalization expenses are covered under the Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana?

  • A. 3 days pre-hospitalization and 15 days post-hospitalization
  • B. 7 days pre-hospitalization and 30 days post-hospitalization
  • C. 5 days pre-hospitalization and 10 days post-hospitalization
  • D. 15 days pre-hospitalization and 30 days post-hospitalization

Answer: 3 days pre-hospitalization and 15 days post-hospitalization

The Ayushman Bharat – Pradhan Mantri Jan Arogya Yojana covers medical expenses incurred up to 3 days prior to hospitalization and up to 15 days post-hospitalization, including diagnostics and medicines.

#106 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the maximum central financial subsidy provided for rooftop solar installation under the Pradhan Mantri Surya Ghar: Muft Bijli Yojana?

  • C. Up to Rupees 1,00,000
  • D. Up to Rupees 30,000

Answer: Up to Rupees 78,000

Under the Pradhan Mantri Surya Ghar: Muft Bijli Yojana, households receive a subsidy of Rupees 30,000 for 1 kilowatt, Rupees 60,000 for 2 kilowatts, and a maximum cap of Rupees 78,000 for rooftop solar systems of 3 kilowatts or higher.

#114 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

The Jal Jeevan Mission operates under the motto 'Har Ghar Nal Se Jal', aiming to supply at least 55 liters of potable water per capita per day to every rural household through functional tap connections.

#115 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the full form of the government initiative GOBARdhan?

  • B. Green Organic Bio-Agro Rural Dhan
  • C. Government Organic Bio-Agricultural Resources Development
  • D. Generating Organic Bio-Agro Renewable Dhan

Answer: Galvanizing Organic Bio-Agro Resources Dhan

GOBARdhan stands for Galvanizing Organic Bio-Agro Resources Dhan, an umbrella initiative aimed at converting cattle dung, agricultural residue, and organic waste into compressed biogas and organic bio-fertilizer.

The Department of Drinking Water and Sanitation under the Ministry of Jal Shakti acts as the nodal coordinating department for the GOBARdhan initiative, working alongside multiple partner ministries.

#118 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

How much annual financial assistance is provided to eligible farmers under the Pradhan Mantri Kisan Samman Nidhi scheme?

  • B. 2015

#120 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the uniform maximum insurance premium payable by farmers under the Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana?

  • A. Kharif: 2%, Rabi: 1.5%, Commercial and Horticultural Crops: 5%
  • B. Kharif: 1.5%, Rabi: 2%, Commercial Crops: 5%
  • C. Kharif: 5%, Rabi: 2%, Commercial Crops: 1.5%
  • D. Uniform 2% for all crops

Answer: Kharif: 2%, Rabi: 1.5%, Commercial and Horticultural Crops: 5%

Under the Pradhan Mantri Fasal Bima Yojana, farmers pay a maximum actuarial premium of 2% for all Kharif food and oilseed crops, 1.5% for all Rabi food and oilseed crops, and 5% for annual commercial and horticultural crops.

#121 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

What is the short-term crop loan limit and effective interest rate for prompt-repaying farmers under the Kisan Credit Card scheme?

  • A. Up to Rupees 3 Lakh at 4% effective interest rate
  • B. Up to Rupees 5 Lakh at 7% effective interest rate
  • C. Up to Rupees 2 Lakh at 3% effective interest rate
  • D. Up to Rupees 1 Lakh at 5% effective interest rate

Answer: Up to Rupees 3 Lakh at 4% effective interest rate

Under the Modified Interest Subvention Scheme for Kisan Credit Card holders, short-term crop loans up to Rupees 3 Lakh carry a 7% benchmark interest rate, with an additional 3% prompt repayment incentive reducing the effective interest rate to 4% per annum.

#122 • Page 2 (Questions 72–132)

In which year was the Soil Health Card scheme launched?

  • A. 2015
  • B. 2014
  • C. 2016

The Viksit Bharat – Guarantee for Rozgar and Ajeevika Mission (Gramin) Act, 2025 replaced the 2005 rural employment framework (commencing nationwide on 1 July 2026) and expanded the statutory employment guarantee to 125 days per rural household annually.

#257 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Ahmedabad in Gujarat earned the sobriquet 'Manchester of India' due to its thriving cotton textile mills along the Sabarmati River, paralleling Manchester's prominence in Great Britain's textile industry.

#263 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

From which glacial lake in Sikkim does the Teesta River originate?

  • B. Gurudongmar Lake, Sikkim
  • C. Gangotri Glacier, Uttarakhand
  • D. Mansarovar Lake, Tibet

Answer: Tso Lhamo Lake (Cholamu Lake), Sikkim

The Teesta River originates from the Tso Lhamo Lake (also known as Cholamu Lake) at an elevation of over 5,300 meters in North Sikkim, fed by the Zemu Glacier.

#264 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

  • A. Brahmaputra River (Jamuna)
  • B. Ganga River (Padma)
  • C. Meghna River
  • D. Barak River

Answer: Brahmaputra River (Jamuna)

After flowing through Sikkim and northern West Bengal, the Teesta River enters Bangladesh and merges with the Brahmaputra River (locally known as the Jamuna) near Fulchhari.

#265 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

The Teesta River water-sharing dispute is between which two countries (and involves which Indian state)?

  • B. India (Assam) and Bhutan
  • C. India (Bihar) and Nepal
  • D. India (Punjab) and Pakistan

Answer: India (West Bengal) and Bangladesh

The Teesta River water-sharing dispute is a longstanding bilateral issue between India (specifically involving the state of West Bengal) and Bangladesh regarding equitable allocation of dry-season river flows.

#266 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Where does the River Ganga originate?

  • A. Gangotri Glacier at Gomukh, Uttarakhand
  • B. Yamunotri Glacier, Uttarakhand
  • C. Mansarovar Lake, Tibet
  • D. Amarkantak Plateau, Madhya Pradesh

Answer: Gangotri Glacier at Gomukh, Uttarakhand

The River Ganga originates as the Bhagirathi from the Gangotri Glacier at Gomukh in Uttarkashi district, Uttarakhand, and takes the name Ganga after merging with the Alaknanda at Devprayag.

#267 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Into which water body does the River Ganga finally empty?

  • A. Bay of Bengal
  • B. Arabian Sea
  • C. Indian Ocean directly
  • D. Gulf of Khambhat

Answer: Bay of Bengal

After traversing over 2,525 kilometers across northern and eastern India and Bangladesh, the Ganga empties into the Bay of Bengal, forming the world's largest delta, the Sundarbans.

#268 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Where does the Yamuna River originate?

  • A. Yamunotri Glacier, Uttarakhand
  • B. Gangotri Glacier, Uttarakhand
  • C. Rohtang Pass, Himachal Pradesh
  • D. Kailash Range, Tibet

Answer: Yamunotri Glacier, Uttarakhand

The Yamuna, the largest tributary of the Ganga, originates from the Yamunotri Glacier near Banderpoonch peaks in Uttarkashi district, Uttarakhand, and joins the Ganga at Triveni Sangam in Prayagraj.

#269 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

From where does the Brahmaputra River originate in Tibet?

  • A. Angsi / Chemayungdung Glacier near Lake Mansarovar, Tibet (known as Yarlung Tsangpo)
  • B. Gangotri Glacier, Uttarakhand
  • C. Amarkantak, Madhya Pradesh
  • D. Tso Lhamo Lake, Sikkim

Answer: Angsi / Chemayungdung Glacier near Lake Mansarovar, Tibet (known as Yarlung Tsangpo)

The Brahmaputra originates near Lake Mansarovar in the Kailash range of southern Tibet, where it flows eastward for over 1,600 kilometers under the name Yarlung Tsangpo before turning south into India.

#270 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Through which Indian state does the Brahmaputra River enter India?

  • A. Arunachal Pradesh
  • B. Assam
  • C. Sikkim
  • D. Nagaland

Answer: Arunachal Pradesh

The Yarlung Tsangpo makes a sharp U-turn around Namcha Barwa peak and enters India through Arunachal Pradesh, where it is known as the Siang or Dihang River before entering Assam.

#271 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Where does the Indus River originate?

  • A. Bokhar Chu Glacier near Lake Mansarovar, Tibet
  • B. Rohtang Pass, Himachal Pradesh
  • C. Verinag Spring, Jammu and Kashmir
  • D. Gangotri Glacier, Uttarakhand

Answer: Bokhar Chu Glacier near Lake Mansarovar, Tibet

The Indus River originates in the Tibetan Plateau in the vicinity of Lake Mansarovar from the Bokhar Chu glacier in the Kailash mountain range.

#272 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

  • B. Himachal Pradesh (India) and Nepal
  • C. Sikkim (India) and Bangladesh
  • D. Uttarakhand (India) and China

Answer: Ladakh (India) and Pakistan

In India, the Indus River flows northwestward exclusively through the Union Territory of Ladakh (passing Leh) before entering Gilgit-Baltistan and flowing southward through Pakistan into the Arabian Sea.

#274 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

  • B. Sorrow of Bengal
  • C. Lifeline of Gujarat
  • D. Vridha Kaveri

Answer: Dakshin Ganga (Ganga of the South)

The Godavari is called 'Dakshin Ganga' (and 'Vridha Ganga') because it is the longest river in Peninsular India (1,465 kilometers) and has the second-largest river basin in India after the Ganga.

#275 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

  • B. Trimbakeshwar, Maharashtra
  • C. Talakaveri, Karnataka
  • D. Multai, Madhya Pradesh

The Krishna River rises in the Western Ghats near Jor village in Mahabaleshwar, Satara district, Maharashtra, and flows through Maharashtra, Karnataka, Telangana, and Andhra Pradesh into the Bay of Bengal.

#276 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Where does the Kaveri River originate?

  • B. Mahabaleshwar, Maharashtra
  • C. Nandi Hills, Karnataka
  • D. Nilgiri Hills, Tamil Nadu

The Kaveri River originates at Talakaveri in the Brahmagiri hills of Kodagu district, Karnataka, and flows southeastward through Karnataka and Tamil Nadu to empty into the Bay of Bengal at Poompuhar.

#277 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

From where does the Narmada River originate?

  • A. Amarkantak Plateau in Anuppur district, Madhya Pradesh
  • B. Multai in Betul district, Madhya Pradesh
  • C. Sihawa, Chhattisgarh
  • D. Trimbakeshwar, Maharashtra

Answer: Amarkantak Plateau in Anuppur district, Madhya Pradesh

The Narmada River rises from the Narmada Kund on the Amarkantak Plateau in Madhya Pradesh, forming the traditional geographic boundary between North and South India.

#278 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

In which direction and into which sea does the Narmada River flow?

  • A. Westwards towards the Arabian Sea
  • B. Eastwards towards the Bay of Bengal
  • C. Northwards to join the Yamuna
  • D. Southwards towards the Indian Ocean

Answer: Westwards towards the Arabian Sea

Unlike most major peninsular rivers that flow east, the Narmada flows westwards through a structural rift valley between the Vindhya and Satpura ranges into the Gulf of Khambhat in the Arabian Sea.

#279 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

Where does the Tapti (Tapi) River originate?

  • A. Multai in Betul district, Madhya Pradesh
  • B. Amarkantak, Madhya Pradesh
  • C. Sihawa, Chhattisgarh
  • D. Mahabaleshwar, Maharashtra

Answer: Multai in Betul district, Madhya Pradesh

The Tapti River originates near Multai in the Satpura range of Betul district, Madhya Pradesh, and flows westwards parallel to the Narmada before draining into the Arabian Sea near Surat.

#280 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

From where does the Mahanadi River originate?

  • A. Sihawa hills in Dhamtari district, Chhattisgarh
  • B. Amarkantak, Madhya Pradesh
  • C. Chota Nagpur Plateau, Jharkhand
  • D. Mahendragiri, Odisha

Answer: Sihawa hills in Dhamtari district, Chhattisgarh

The Mahanadi River rises from the Sihawa mountain range in Dhamtari district, Chhattisgarh, and flows eastwards through Odisha, where the famous Hirakud Dam is built across it.

Madhya Pradesh has the highest number of National Parks in India with 11 protected National Parks, including Kanha, Bandhavgarh, Pench, Satpura, Panna, and Kuno.

#282 • Page 3 (Questions 133–290)

  • B. Madhya Pradesh; Largest National Park in India
  • C. Assam; First Biosphere Reserve in India
  • D. Gujarat; Only habitat of Asiatic Lions

Answer: Uttarakhand; India's first National Park (established in 1936)

Located in Nainital and Pauri Garhwal districts of Uttarakhand, Jim Corbett National Park was established in 1936 as Hailey National Park, making it India's oldest and first National Park.

  • B. West Bengal; Royal Bengal Tiger
  • C. Gujarat; Asiatic Lion
  • D. Kerala; Nilgiri Tahr

Answer: Assam; Great Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros

Kaziranga National Park is a United Nations Educational, Scientific and Cultural Organization World Heritage Site in Assam that hosts the world's largest population of the Great Indian One-Horned Rhinoceros.

  • D. Karnataka; Indian Leopard

Answer: Gujarat; Asiatic Lion

Gir National Park and Wildlife Sanctuary in Gujarat is the sole natural wilderness habitat in the world where the endangered Asiatic Lion survives.

  • D. Gujarat; Wild Ass

Sundarbans National Park in West Bengal is part of the world's largest tidal halophytic mangrove forest in the Ganges-Brahmaputra delta and is a designated habitat for the Royal Bengal Tiger.

Hemis National Park in the Union Territory of Ladakh spans approximately 4,400 square kilometers, making it the largest National Park in India and a prime habitat for the elusive Snow Leopard.

#327 • Page 4 (Questions 291–351)

Kanchenjunga, located on the border of Sikkim and Nepal at an elevation of 8,586 meters, is the highest peak within administered Indian territory and the third-highest mountain globally.

The Border Roads Organisation was formed on 7 May 1960 with its headquarters at Seema Sadak Bhawan in New Delhi to secure India's borders and develop infrastructure in remote north and northeastern border states.

  • C. Cabinet Council on Economic Affairs
  • D. Committee on Corporate and Economic Administration

Answer: Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs

CCEA stands for Cabinet Committee on Economic Affairs, a key standing committee of the Union Cabinet chaired by the Prime Minister that reviews economic trends, agricultural Minimum Support Prices, and major infrastructure approvals.

Which of the following is Earth's only natural satellite?

  • B. Indian National Satellite System (INSAT)
  • C. Global Positioning System (GPS)
  • D. Hubble Space Telescope

Answer: Moon

The Moon is Earth's only natural satellite, orbiting at an average distance of about 384,400 kilometers and stabilizing Earth's axial tilt.
